‘2001: A Space Odyssey’ movie prop may sell for over $1 million

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 3, 2026 12:00 PM EST

Now is your chance to own as many pieces of film history as your bank account will allow. Numerous items from filmmaker Stanley Kubrick’s personal archive are hitting the auction block for the first time. Equipment, props, scripts, and more will go up for bid directly from the Stanley Kubrick Film Archives at Bonhams in London this February.

Stanley Kubrick (1928-1999) was one of Hollywood’s most respected and influential filmmakers. His most impactful films include 2001: A Space Odyssey (1968), A Clockwork Orange (1971), The Shining (1980), and Eyes Wide Shut (1999). His films pushed boundaries and left a lasting impression that extended far beyond the screen.

“His movies have stood the test of time and will continue to be relevant to our shared humanity as long as people love the art of great cinema,” Katharina Kubrick, an actor and Kubrick’s daughter, said in a statement. “We are proud to be able to share with you some of the items from this unique collection, which we hope will find new homes and be cherished until it is time to pass them on to the next generation of film lovers.”

The folding dark wood director’s chair accompanied Kubrick throughout his career. It has a pale green canvas seat and back, and each of the arms has a customised script box attached to it and the name “Stanley Kubrick” stencilled in black. It is estimated to sell for $11,000 to $16,000.

The ghostly Grady sisters Danny are amongst the most scary elements in Kubrick’s 1980 film film The Shining. Costume designer Milena Canonero said Kubrick wanted the girls to wear matching outfits that wouldn’t tie them to a specific period. Canonero took inspiration from clothing store catalogues and said she had a “1940s American style” in mind for the Grady sisters. These are expected to sell for $54,000 to $80,000

2001: A Space Odyssey (1968) is widely regarded as Kubrick’s masterpiece. The Star Child has a brief appearance at the conclusion of the film, but is highly significant. This fiberglass baby became a memorable image from the film that won the Academy Award for Best Special Effects in 1969. This lot is expected to sell for $800,000 to $1,100,000.

Kubrick had a well-documented passion for chess, and he often applied lessons he learned from the game’s discipline into his filmmaking. He developed his love for the game during his teens in New York City’s Washington Square Park, playing for hours against other chess enthusiasts and local hustlers. This chess set is estimated to sell for $2,700 to $5,400.

“Few people have had as much of an overwhelming impact on visual culture as Stanley Kubrick,” added Claire Tole-Moir, Head of Popular Culture at Bonhams. “His films provide such a striking assortment of distinctive images, that you never forget your first time watching them. His precise, bold style and technical mastery is so distinctive that the word ‘Kubrickian’ even entered the English dictionary.”

All of the items up for auction can be viewed at Bonhams website. They can also be seen in person at Bonhams Los Angeles (November 2 to November 6) and Bonhams Paris (November 16, 17, 21, 23, and 24). The live auction will take place at Bonhams in London on Wednesday,  February 3, 2027, with an online auction ending on February 4 at 12 p.m. local time.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/measles-update-us-passes-4000-cases-pennsylvania-reports-sixth-death" hreflang="en">Measles update: New York reports first measles-associated death; US nears 5,000 confirmed cases</a>

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Measles update: New York reports first measles-associated death; US nears 5,000 confirmed cases" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The New York State Department of Health on Monday afternoon reported its first measles-associated death in 30 years.

The state has counted 210 total cases this year as of Nov. 3. The reported death was a child in the northern Lewis County, according to the department. No information was given about the child's specific illness or the role measles infection may have played in their death.

Sixteen confirmed cases of measles have been reported in Lewis County as of Tuesday, the department said. Many of New York's cases are located among upstate counties, and just eight are in New York City, according to the department.

In a statement, New York Governor Kathy Hochul—who on Oct. 5 declared a Disaster Emergency in response to rising measles case rates—expressed her sympathies to the child's family and told residents that "vaccination remains our best protection against this dangerous disease."

"This is New York’s first measles-related death in 30 years, a devastating reminder of what’s at stake when Washington deliberately erodes decades of progress in protecting Americans from preventable illnesses," the statement reads, echoing criticisms of federal health leadership voiced by Pennsylvania Governor Josh Shapiro regarding measles-associated deaths in his own state.

As of Oct. 29, the Centers for Disease Control reports 4,852 confirmed cases of measles this year across the country, far outpacing 2025's total of 2,289. Of these, 511 have been hospitalized (versus 244 in 2025) and three deaths have been recognized—though health officials in Pennsylvania alone said they have confirmed five measles-associated deaths. The CDC has said its death count may change after it completes work to develop a new standardized case definition for "deaths due to measles."

The U.S. is in the midst of its worst measles outbreak in decades, with more than 4,000 cases reported to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention as of early October and a sixth measles-associated death disclosed by local Pennsylvania officials on Monday.

The vaccine-preventable disease was declared eliminated in the U.S. back in 2000—meaning that there was no endemic spread of the disease—but has resurfaced in force during recent years as declining childhood vaccination rates have pushed most states below the 95% coverage bar for school-age herd immunity.

The CDC had counted 2,289 cases across the entirety of 2025, which had been the country’s highest total since 1990, and several public health officials have warned for months that number would likely be surpassed based on the scale and frequency of early-year cases.

Measles-associated deaths have also accompanied the higher case counts, though just how many is a subject of dispute. While three deaths were confirmed in all of 2025, this year logged its first in late August with Pennsylvania, which hosts more than a quarter of the total cases, now reporting six measles-associated deaths as of Oct. 13. The CDC, however, lists two deaths alongside a note that its count is subject to change, and has promised more broadly to develop a new, standardized case definition to be used across all reporting states.

That discrepancy is an example of friction between federal health leadership and the broader public health community that experts say has contributed to this year’s outbreaks.

Pennsylvania’s initial death announcements in August were contested by Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr., who accused Pennsylvania Governor Josh Shapiro of withholding sufficient information for the CDC to confirm the deaths and using his press conference announcing them as “gaslighting the public” for political purposes.

During an ensuing social media feud, the governor and the secretary described a heated phone call in which Shapiro (at the time) rejected federal assistance and accused Kennedy of fueling the outbreaks with only lukewarm endorsement of the MMR vaccine and a broader concurrent campaign to scale back other routine childhood vaccinations.

Numerous state public health officials and experts have lobbed similar criticisms of Kennedy, who is affiliated with anti-vaccine groups, and federal health agencies under his purview.

The CDC, for its part, has reportedly infused at least $100 million of extra public health funding to states for immunization programs and surveillance, and leading officials including Kennedy and new CDC Director Erica Schwartz, M.D., have described the MMR vaccine as safe and effective when asked directly. Still, the agency has not launched the type of broader vaccine messaging and awareness campaigns that it would have in years past, critics are quick to note.

Pennsylvania isn’t alone in raising alarms over rising measles cases. New York Governor Kathy Hochul declared a State Disaster Emergency in early October, the first such declaration in recent years.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/digital-health/hinge-health-posted-52-revenue-growth-q3-it-ramps-expansion-migraine-gi-care" hreflang="en">Hinge Health revenue jumps 52% in Q3 as it expands beyond MSK, eyes hardware acquisitions</a>

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Hinge Health revenue jumps 52% in Q3 as it expands beyond MSK, eyes hardware acquisitions" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Hinge Health is seeing early traction with its migraine and gastrointestinal care programs as it pushes beyond musculoskeletal care and builds a broader, multi-condition platform.

But the company's core MSK business remains the engine fueling its robust growth.

"Our vision has always been to automate the delivery of care using technology to close the gap between the care people need and the care they actually receive. We're now applying that same approach and the same trusted relationships with employers and partners in neurology and gastrointestinal care," Daniel Perez, Hinge Health co-founder and CEO, told analysts and investors on the third-quarter earnings call Monday. "We're not starting from scratch in each new category. We're extending a platform that's already proven into conditions that are often connected to the ones we already treat. We believe that's a fundamentally different and more powerful way to expand than what other companies in our space are attempting."

During a Q&A with analysts and investors on the earnings call, Perez asserted, "We’re not taking our eye off the ball in MSK. I think we’re actually widening our lead on MSK… But it’s allowing us to expand into other areas as well."

In the third quarter, the company posted $235 million in revenue, up 52% from $154 million a year ago, and above its guidance range of $223 million to $225 million. The company posted third-quarter adjusted earnings of 66 cents per share, exceeding Wall Street analysts' estimates of 60 cents per share. Hinge Health's non-GAAP income from operations increased 154% to $77.2 million in the third quarter.

Hinge Health, which launched in late 2014 and went public in May 2025, developed software combined with AI to automate physical therapy services for joint and muscle health. The company has designed its platform to address a broad spectrum of MSK care, including acute injury, chronic pain and post-surgical rehabilitation. To address the automation of care, Hinge has developed AI-powered motion tracking technology, a proprietary FDA-cleared wearable device called Enso and an AI-supported care team to deliver scalable and personalized MSK care.

The company reported strong client growth, with 3,270 clients as of September 30, up 28% from a year ago. Last twelve months (LTM) calculated billings increased 54% year-over-year to $960 million as of September 30, compared to $624 million during Q3 2025.

The company also benefited from strong operational efficiency as it reported gross margin of 87% in Q3, compared to 82% during the same quarter a year ago.

Free cash flow for the quarter was $113 million, representing a 51% free cash flow margin. The company ended the September quarter with total cash and equivalents of $359 million.

Based on strong Q3 and year-to-date results, Hinge Health boosted its 2026 outlook, now expecting full-year revenue to reach between $879 million and $881 million, reflecting year-over-year growth of 50% at the midpoint. That compares to the prior revenue target of $856 million to $860 million. The company now projects non-GAAP income from operations to be between $266 million and $268 million, reflecting year-over-year growth of 123% and non-GAAP operating margin of 30% at the midpoint.

Core MSK is still the company's largest revenue contributor by a wide margin, Perez noted, and that business is growing robustly. "We expect it to remain that way for years to come; the opportunity at MSK remains massively underpenetrated," Perez said.

"The MSK opportunity is still enormous. Musculoskeletal conditions are among the leading drivers of disability and healthcare spend in the United States, and the vast majority of people in America still don't have access to a digital MSK solution," Perez said during the call.

"We built something that works clinically, commercially, and economically, and we're still in the early innings of penetrating that market. We are the undisputed leader in this space, and we estimate 88% of lives within our markets are yet to be won, leaving a long runway for growth. That alone is a multi-year growth opportunity," he said.

The company is building on top of that MSK care foundation as it expands into migraine care and gastrointestinal care. Hinge acquired GI startup Cylinder Health in August for $105 million in cash.

The company's client base is up almost 30% year-over-year for its MSK care program and roughly 40% for its new GI Care Program since the acquisition announcement of Cylinder Health in August, Perez noted.

The company expects to make more acquisitions and is even looking at hardware acquisitions, Perez said. "I think too many technology companies, especially in healthcare, focus exclusively on software, but I absolutely firmly believe you will not automate care delivery without also investing in hardware. And, we've shown we're pretty good at hardware," he said, as the company internally developed its Enso wearable device.

Perez told Bloomberg last month that the company was interested in hardware that can augment virtual care.

"We will build organically first and use M&A when it accelerates our timeline. And our acquisitions, particularly in hardware, have historically been small tuck-ins of technology in a team, and then you build it out into something much larger," he added.

Looking at the company's new care programs, Perez called out notable early results from Hinge's migraine program, which is still in early access: 23% reduction in monthly migraine days, 28% reduction in migraine duration, equivalent to roughly five hours and just over half of surveyed members achieved a clinically meaningful improvement.

"We're also seeing a meaningful reduction in monthly prescription use days, which matters both for members and for the clients paying for their care," Perez said.

More than 800 clients, representing 9 million lives, now have access to the migraine program, approximately double the previous quarter, according to management. The migraine program was announced in May.

For the GI care business, Hinge Health is taking Cylinder's existing program and building it into the same app and care infrastructure it uses for MSK and migraine. The company is targeting summer 2027 for the fully integrated product. Meaningful revenue contribution should emerge particularly in 2028, after Hinge gets a fuller sales cycle with the integrated product, Perez noted.

Hinge Health sees an opportunity to offer health plans and employers one integrated care platform that covers multiple health conditions rather than separate point solutions.

Nearly one in two people with GI conditions also have MSK pain, and 70% of people withmigraine also have a GI condition, Perez noted.

"GI cost has been a growing pain point for employers and health plans, especially with the growth of GLP-1s, given GI side effects are so common, and our employer clients are very grateful for taking this on," he said during the call.

Hinge Health had a strong selling season in Q3, according to Aaryn Pure, Hinge Health's chief commercial officer, with three new Fortune 15 clients. "We've already received verbal agreements on more lives this year than we closed in all of last year, and last year was a record-breaking sales year for adding eligible lives," Pure noted.

More than 10 million lives across more than 800 clients have access to multiple Hinge products, he said.

Small and midsize businesses are becoming an important growth channel for the company, even though Hinge began investing in that market just a year ago. Through Q3, Hinge Health closed nearly 1 million SMB lives, "demonstrating we're not just winning at the top of the market, we're adding clients across every size tier," Pure said on the call.

He also noted that some health plan and third-party administrator (TPA) partners are implementing or considering arrangements where Hinge Health is automatically embedded or offered on an opt-out basis in their SMB book of business.

"Being the first-mover preferred partner is allowing us to capture that market," Pure said.

The company also announced Monday that Jim Pursley will step down as president, effective January 1, and will join Hinge Health's board of directors. Following this transition, Pure will lead the commercial organization, the company said.

In an analyst note, Ryan Daniels, group head of healthcare technology and services at William Blair, wrote that Hinge Health demonstrates "market leadership with significant advantages both on the product and go-to-market fronts."

"With a nearly 150% increase in shares YTD, HNGE now trades at approximately 11 times our 2027 adjusted gross profit, a premium to mature, high-margin, growth-oriented healthcare IT peers. Still, at these levels, we believe the stock presents a compelling long-term risk/reward and see continued growth momentum and upside to operating income and free cash flow estimates," Daniels wrote.

Matthias throwing his players under the bus is a dangerous game. Newcastle have no identity, no direction and no patterns - and that's on the manager, writes CRAIG HOPE

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Published: 05:50 EDT, 3 November 2026 | Updated: 11:58 EDT, 3 November 2026

There often comes a point in a manager’s reign when the blame for defeat shifts from the dugout to the dressing room, and it is invariably the point of no return. Rarely does it arrive after just nine matches.

So when Matthias Jaissle took aim at his players in the wake of Newcastle’s 1-0 defeat by Everton on Monday, it was arguably a more concerning display than the 90 minutes which had preceded it, as alarming as those had been.

Jaissle’s post-match reflections were not inaccurate. This was not a manager talking nonsense. He fronted up and gave what felt like honest answers.

The 38-year-old is bright and articulate and, with each defeat, has shown an increasing humility that was less visible during the optimism of his introductory days.

To show the players the underside of the bus so soon, however, is as baffling a tactic as the strategy enacted on the pitch, even if Jaissle insists that performance had no resemblance to what he had asked of his team.

Rather, says the German, he had wanted his players to keep the ball on the floor and get the wingers in one-on-one situations high up the pitch. What he saw was a collection of long balls and a heading-practice session for Everton’s centre backs. The wingers, meanwhile, were spectators.

When a manager blames the players for defeat - as Matthias Jaissle did after Newcastle lost to Everton on Monday night - it is often the beginning of the end for the man in the dugout

Newcastle winger Harvey Barnes struggled to get on the ball during the defeat by Everton

‘The plan was not to shoot the long balls,’ he said.

Nor was the plan to shoot at all, it seemed. It took until the 85th minute for Newcastle to trouble Jordan Pickford, and even then the goalkeeper wasn’t overly concerned.

Short ball? Long ball? Whatever pass Newcastle pick, it is invariably the wrong ball. They are a team without identity – no patterns, no partnerships and, certainly, no sign of progress. So while there is some sympathy with Jaissle if players are failing to follow his instructions, it always comes back on any manager to make sure they do.

I once wrote of Everton boss David Moyes, when in charge of Sunderland, that he had inherited a sinking ship, only to drill another hole. Moyes, for the record, is a very good manager, he just wasn’t at Sunderland.

And that is the danger with Jaissle. He, too, walked into a difficult situation post Eddie Howe and the loss of several star players. Their replacements have been bench-warners or cold starters.

But Jaissle has to be the solution, not another problem. At present, the fear is that a young manager with no major-league experience is held hostage by his ideals and refuses to bend to what his players want and need.

His continued use of a 4-2-3-1 system, with two midfielders who cannot really run and a revolving cast of No 10s who are not No 10s, feels with each passing week and each passing defeat like a blueprint for relegation.

Maybe the team ignoring their manager’s wishes on Monday night was a cry for change. A protest, even? Do they not believe in his methods or do they not understand them?

Newcastle's Yoane Wissa rues a missed chance against Everton in what was Newcastle's third loss in their last five Premier League games

Jaissle tries to get his message across to Joelinton and Harvey Barnes at St James' Park

Either way, Newcastle must try something different. The Everton loss, and the nature of a performance absent of direction or conviction, was not a surprise. This run of form – four defeats in five in all competitions – has been coming since the early weeks of the season, when results flattered performances and the underlying numbers told a more troubling story.

It is not the time to change manager, and nor do the club’s hierarchy think so. The change has to come from within – approach, attitude, execution. That is on Jaissle and the players, not one or the other.

That is why before the head coach addresses what happened on the pitch, he must clarify to his players what he said off it. To lose matches is one thing, but to risk losing your dressing room is quite another.

That would leave Jaissle facing the one situation from which his ‘vertical football’ could offer no way forward.

GLP-1s May Help Lower Diabetes, Heart Risks Better Than Standard Prevention

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

GLP-1 drugs are more effective in helping prevent type 2 diabetes than the commonly prescribed medication metformin, according to a new study.

Researchers said GLP-1s such as Ozempic and Mounjaro were more likely to reduce blood sugar to healthy levels in people with prediabetes and obesity.

They added that people prescribed GLP-1 medications had a lower risk of progressing from prediabetes to type 2 diabetes.

The researchers noted that GLP-1 users also had a lower risk of death from any cause as well as fewer heart attacks, strokes, and heart failure events compared with metformin users.

“The great news with prediabetes is that it’s still possible to reverse the progression of the disease,” said Vinicius de Sousa Barzon Serra, MD, a study author and research fellow in cardiovascular medicine at the Mayo Clinic in Scottsdale, Arizona, in a press release. “This research may add to the case for considering GLP-1 medications earlier for certain patients, rather than waiting until they develop type 2 diabetes.”

Serra noted that metformin still remains an effective treatment for people with prediabetes and type 2 diabetes.

“This is less about replacing one medication with another and more about discussing additional options with specific patients,” he said.

The researchers presented their findings at the American Heart Association Scientific Sessions 2026 in Chicago. The research has not been published yet in a peer-reviewed journal.

David Cutler, MD, a family medicine physician at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, CA, wasn’t involved in the study, but said the findings were “quite impressive.”

“It elevates the comfort of prescribing GLP-1 drugs as a front-line treatment,” he told Healthline.

Robert Eckel, MD, a former president of the American Heart Association who was likewise not involved in the study, agrees the research provides more options for patients and prescribers.

“GLP-1s provide another option for prevention, perhaps as an add-on therapy. However, costs and access to these medications are important factors,” said Eckel, an endocrinologist at the University of Colorado School of Medicine, in the press release.

For their study, the researchers analyzed the medical records of 256,000 adults in the United States with prediabetes and obesity. About 76% of the subjects were women, and the average age of the participants was 52.

About half of those studied were taking GLP-1 drugs with the active ingredient semaglutide, tirzepatide, liraglutide, or dulaglutide.

The other half had been prescribed metformin, a medication used to help manage blood sugar levels in adults and some children with type 2 diabetes by decreasing glucose production in the liver, reducing glucose absorption from food, and improving the body’s insulin sensitivity.

The researchers reported that the people in the study who were taking GLP-1 drugs had a 1.5 times higher chance of reversing high blood sugar to healthy levels compared to people who were using metformin.

The researchers also noted that GLP-1 medication users had a 31% lower risk of developing type 2 diabetes and a 41% lower risk of death from any cause.

They added that GLP-1 medications were associated with 15% fewer heart attacks, 20% fewer strokes, 25% fewer cases of heart failure, and 8% fewer cases of atrial fibrillation (AFib), a common type of irregular heartbeat.

The researchers cautioned that their study is observational and, therefore, cannot prove a cause-and-effect relationship. They also noted they relied on prescription records, so the frequency and dosage at which patients took their medication was not reflected.

In addition, the GLP-1 drugs were grouped together, so individual differences between medications were not considered.

Eckel said in the news release that the benefits for people taking GLP-1 drugs were probably mainly due to weight loss. Eckel noted that the research results open up a wide range of possibilities.

“This is an important finding because the GLP-1 receptor agonists are commonly used today and this is a population that in the transition from prediabetes to diabetes are at increasing risk for cardiovascular disease,” Eckel said in a taped interview alongside the study findings.

Cutler pointed out that several GLP-1 drugs also target blood sugar levels and insulin production. They could be effective in people with prediabetes who don’t have weight management issues.

Cutler noted that these people, however, might be reluctant to take GLP-1 drugs because they may not be experiencing symptoms of their prediabetes but could develop side effects with the medications.

“They would not be getting any immediate benefits,” Cutler said. He added that metformin, which was first used clinically in 1958, remains an effective alternative. “It’s been proven safe, effective, and it’s very cheap,” Cutler said.

It’s estimated that 40 million people in the United States have diabetes, with more than 90% of those cases being type 2 diabetes, a chronic condition characterized by high blood glucose levels that results from the body’s ineffective response to insulin or insufficient insulin production.

It’s also estimated that 115 million adults in the United States have prediabetes, a condition in which a person has higher-than-normal blood sugar levels but not high enough to be diagnosed as type 2 diabetes.

Around 5–10% of people with prediabetes develop diabetes every year. More broadly, up to 70% of people with prediabetes eventually develop diabetes.

Medication is sometimes prescribed for people with type 2 diabetes and prediabetes. Lifestyle changes can help reverse type 2 diabetes and prediabetes, as well as prevent the progression from prediabetes to type 2 diabetes.

Cutler said the lifestyle changes needed to prevent type 2 diabetes are pretty clear.

“The most important thing for people with prediabetes to know is they should lose weight, reduce their carbs, exercise daily and, if that doesn’t work, then consider medication,” he said.
